ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u7	We begin with the debate on the validation or repeal of Royal Decree-Law 4/2020 of 18 February, which repeals the objective dismissal for lack of labour assistance provided for in Article 52(d) of the Consolidated Text of the Act on the Status of Workers, approved by Royal Legislative Decree 2/2015 of 23 October. To present the Royal Decree-Law, the Minister of Labour and Social Economy has the floor on behalf of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u8	Good afternoon. Thank you, President. Just over a month ago, the Royal Decree-Law repealing Article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute was published in the State Official Gazette, which today we bring to debate and vote in this House for validation. As you know, this royal decree-law repeals the article that allowed, to date, the objective dismissal for lack of work attendance including absences for common justified diseases. This was like this until a couple of months ago. Even if it seems to be a lie, in Spain a worker who did not attend work could be dismissed for being ill, even if he had a medical justification. That objective cause of dismissal existed until 2012 and the 2012 labour reform facilitated its use. Today we find it difficult to understand from a work point of view, from a public and business health point of view. First, to heal and then to work for the good of everyone. This is the first duty of working people when they are sick. Never, under any circumstances, go to work sick or sick for fear of being fired. Take care, avoid contact with other people and heal. We knew then, but now that evidence has become an absolute, unquestionable truth, when we face the global health crisis caused by the coronavirus. Today, when we talk about protecting one's own health and public health, we are more aware of the absurdity of this article 52.d). Today, knowledge about occupational health is not limited to unknown WHO recommendations or health prevention circulars. We've learned. We have always known that health is one of our most precious assets, but unfortunately, today we know that self-care also guarantees the protection of others. It's not coming from now. Health authorities in all countries and in all political colours have set standards for sick people not to attend their jobs, for their own health and to avoid spread. And it is that in reality the most common causes of absence from work are viral diseases of easy contagion. This is what it is about, that staying at home because you are sick cannot be a reason for dismissal because, moreover, in many cases it is not just a health recommendation for the person but for public health. Article 52(d) of the Workers'Statute involved an attack on the health of workers who were forced to attend work against their will, which could exacerbate their illness. It sought the basic principles of occupational safety and health by turning workplaces into places at greater risk of disease, and was systematically attacking public health by inducing sick people to go to places where they can spread their disease. Because this measure is also negative for companies. If we force sick people to go to work we can cause the opposite effect and the number of casualties is much higher. But we are not just talking about health, we are talking about dismissals based on unfair causes and this also violates fundamental rights. No one can be fired for justified absences. We have said this from the outset, fundamental rights cannot be touched and are not subject to negotiation. These are historical conquests of rights. In the same way that we have managed to limit working hours or the right to a retirement pension, it had also been achieved that they would not be able to fire you for illness. With this royal decree-law we are, in short, recovering rights; we are ending up with a Spanish anomaly in the field of work, an unjustifiable situation that has no place in any legal system of our environment. With this royal decree-law we are adapting the Spanish legislation to the rules and jurisprudence of the Union and removing from our unfortunate order vestiges of the past. In any case, it is true that the target dismissal for lack of assistance was an already old figure, coming from the first drafting of the 1980 Workers' Statute, and fortunately, very little used in the past. It could be said that the target dismissal for lack of assistance has remained in force in the Spanish legal system during these forty years because it barely affected, given its limited use, but everything changed when the labour reform of 2012 made a slight change in its formulation that, suddenly, caused this kind of dismissal to begin to be used. In practice, this derogation implies restoring a right abolished by the 2012 labour reform. What has happened since that reform is well known. It was simply facilitated that this cause of dismissal could be used by the employer. Before 2012, as you know, two requirements were required: a volume of the worker's non-attendance and, in addition, a volume of overall non-attendance was required in the company. This second element, the volume of global inactivity, served to reflect that the company was suffering material damage from absenteeism. But with the 2012 reform, this second requirement disappeared, which precisely measured the proportionality of a measure as drastic as dismissal. The outcome is known: there has been an increase in the use of layoffs for lack of assistance. What was previously an invisible norm has become an effective formula for firing workers. There are many indications that we can prove this drastic increase in the number of layoffs for this cause. In fact, the objective dismissal for absenteeism has been an uncomfortable figure since it was invented forty years ago and was discussed in this very Congress of Deputies. In the Diary of Sessions of December 18, 1979, there is what the then deputy Felipe González literally said about this dismissal: Here it is a question of consecrating the original sin in the Statute of the Workers. As a number of workers sin, they have to pay all the remaining workers. And that's exactly what this is, that they pay righteous for sinners. If a few abuse we act on this abuse, not on the whole. It is indecency to regard all working people as suspects. Felipe González himself added in his speech: This is a legal monstrosity and an absolute lack of equity. In short, we believe that this is the first time that the fight against absenteeism has been introduced throughout the world by a procedure like this. It was 1979. This legal monstrosity had to be corrected over the years in order, precisely, to exclude absences that could lead to this dismissal and that were totally intolerable. So it happened, as you know, with accidents at work, with maternity leave, with holidays. The last exclusion was precisely the absences from gender-based violence incorporated precisely in 2012. In the end, the only absences that allowed the application of the target dismissal due to lack of assistance were unjustified, and casualties due to common disease lasting less than twenty days, i.e., the most common. But the possibility of someone being fired legitimately because he accumulates casualties due to common disease lasting less than twenty days makes no sense. At the outset, both the fraud of the affected person and the malfunctioning of the medical discharge system without evidence and without alternatives are presumed. There is also another major problem: targeted dismissal due to lack of assistance harms the most vulnerable people, particularly people with disabilities or basic chronic diseases, who are more likely to have a higher number of short-term medical casualties precisely as a result of these diseases. It is in this area that our country has been warned by the Court of Justice of the European Union that Article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute contravenes Directive 2000/78 on equal treatment in employment and occupation. This was established routinely by the ECJ in its judgment of 18 January 2018 in the Ruiz Conejero case. In this judgment, the ECJ warned Spain that the target dismissal for lack of assistance was discriminatory on the grounds of disability. The Spanish rule, the court said, involved indirect discrimination on the grounds of disability, given the higher incidence of short-term falls in persons with disabilities. The judgement also indicated that measures such as this, which may have perverse effects on persons with disabilities, could only be considered legitimate if they were intended to combat absenteeism and were not beyond what is strictly necessary. But the Spanish dismissal, of article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute, does not meet these requirements nor is it adequate to combat absenteeism or has limits to ensure its proportionality; when a fundamental right such as the right to non-discrimination on the grounds of disability is at stake, it has no place. The judgment against Spain in the Ruiz Conejero case is not an isolated judgment; you know that, for years now, the ECJ has established that dismissals for lack of assistance are indirect discrimination in other judgments, e.g. that of 11 April 2003, the Danmark case, and also, recently, that of 11 September 2019. There is therefore no option: Article 52(d) of the Staff Regulations must be removed from the Spanish legal system simply because it is contrary to Directive 2000/78. They are also aware that a few months ago the Constitutional Court ruled on the objective dismissal for lack of assistance and considered that it was suited to the Spanish Constitution; it did so in Judgment 118/2019 of 16 October. Target dismissal for lack of assistance should not only be removed from the legal system because it is harmful to persons with disabilities, it also has a perverse effect on other vulnerable groups such as people with long-term illnesses and people with dependants. There is another particularly vulnerable group in the labour market that may be affected by the target dismissal for lack of assistance; it is the group of persons responsible for the care of dependents that, as is well known and statistically observed, is composed mostly of women. It would appear that our article 52(d) respected conciliation rights because it expressly stated that parental leave would be out of the question for the purposes of dismissal, but it is clear that this does not solve the most common problems. Indeed, people with care responsibilities face hardly foreseeable situations; it is the greater family force that we so well know who we have had to pick up a daughter with a fever at school or attend to an older person, for example, because she has fallen. In the Union Directive 1158/2019 - which you are aware of and will be implemented as soon as possible - states must establish mechanisms to deal with the greater family force in situations that make the presence of the working person indispensable. It is clear that this obligation is not properly fulfilled if the persons who are to serve this greater family force are penalized. Moreover, if dismissal of sick persons is considered legitimate, solidarity between citizens is being undermined and the risk of exclusion of particularly vulnerable persons is being increased, as well as the right to health of persons is being jeopardized. But, regardless of legal qualifications, there are two fundamental principles that are unquestionable: on the one hand, that no one can be punished for exercising their rights and, on the other, that our legal system cannot legitimize behaviours that increase the risk of social exclusion of the most vulnerable groups. This is contrary not only to our international commitments as a country, but also to our commitments as a democratic society. The statement so far shows that the disappearance of our legal order from the objective dismissal for lack of assistance under article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute, must be done immediately by means of the royal decree-law for attending the extraordinary and urgent need proclaimed by article 86.1 of the Spanish Constitution. Firstly, it is a figure which contradicts what we have said in Directive 2078 on the establishment of a general framework for equal treatment in employment and occupation, because, as we have pointed out, it constitutes indirect discrimination on the grounds of disability - the Ruiz Conjero case -. By applying the principle of primacy of Union legislation, it is an obligation of the Member States that domestic legislation conforms to directives within the time limit for transposition. Since the deadline for transposition of Directive 2078 has already passed far, the maintenance of this figure could imply for the Spanish State the application of penalties for non-compliance, hence the urgency becomes inevitable. In addition, persons who in Spain had been injured by the maintenance of this form of dismissal could claim against the Spanish State for the damage suffered in breach of the directive. As you know, this is the doctrine emanating from the Francovich Sentence, which was dictated on November 19, 1991. In short, the failure to bring domestic legislation into line with European Union law must be corrected immediately, since the primacy of European Union law is a basic principle which is endowed with important measures to be effective. Secondly, the reform that took place in 2012 in the formulation of the target dismissal for lack of labour assistance has broadened the assumptions and led to an increase in the number of such redundancies. This, in itself, being a figure contrary to European Union law, reinforces the need for its immediate abolition. In addition, there has recently been an increase in judicial pronouncements on the matter which, as you know, do not always have identical content, which threatens legal certainty, which is a relevant constitutional principle to be preserved. There is therefore a necessary connection between the emergency situation set out above and the concrete measure taken to repeal Article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute, as it is necessary to eliminate immediately the situation which could lead to discrimination, legal uncertainty and non-compliance with the obligations arising from European law. It is necessary and urgent. In conclusion, I would like to tell you that the repeal of Article 52(d) was the first commitment of our government programme and came into force one month after it took possession. We've done it. It was a demand of an immense majority of citizens, I believe a cry in our country and, the Government of Spain, has complied with the citizenship. It was a requirement of European legislation, to end a work and democratic anomaly and thus ensure compliance with the directives. We have complied with Europe. It was a guarantee of fundamental rights to place health, care and non-discrimination at the heart of labour legislation. We have also complied with the Spanish Constitution, but above all we have complied with the millions of working people who lived under the threat of a possible dismissal. There's no right for them to fire you for getting sick. Today more than ever the right to health, the right that if you get sick no one can fire you for it. Right and responsibility so that sick people do not spread their disease. Public health and own health. The whole House is aware that there is no point in retaining Article 52(d). In fact, we have no record that anyone has defended the continuity of this precept. Let us act from the present and for the future. I ask you, on behalf of the Government of Spain, to vote in favour of this royal decree-law, looking forward, thinking about what we have learned in these weeks, a positive vote. It is time to value our health, to value our labour law, to protect the most vulnerable; it is time to give tranquillity and legal protection to all people who live from their work. After what we have learned in these days, let us send a clear and united message to our society: If you are sick, stay home. We owe it to ourselves and ourselves; we owe it to health professionals, who today in hospitals and clinics across the country give all their effort and dedication to healing us. Let us value our health today more than ever, defend it as our most precious good and listen to what people tell us every day from all the windows and balconies of our country. Thank you very much, and I hope you will give a positive vote to this royal decree. [[Applauds]]

Article 52 (d) of the Workers' Statute does not seek to confront companies and employees, it does not seek arbitrary dismissal; for this purpose the judicial avenue of challenge is provided. The aim is to take measures to balance the protection of workers and the viability of companies against abuse. This issue should be dealt with without haste, without resorting to the way of the decree-law, in a calm debate and with the social partners, trade unions and business organisations, because this would make it possible to advance the necessary balance of labour relations, trying to deal reasonably with the damage that absenteeism causes to the productivity of enterprises and, consequently, to their ability to create employment. We would like to know whether you are aware of the problem of absenteeism at work and, if so, what measures you intend to take. If they do not know, absenteeism has an annual cost of EUR 71 million, an insurmountable cost for many of the small and medium-sized enterprises, which represent 99% of Spain's business fabric. In addition, in 2012 the protection of workers was strengthened by excluding from article 52(d) situations of pregnant women or persons with serious illnesses, for which in no case could the lack of assistance to justify dismissal be considered. There has been recent case law on these matters, as you have said, both European and Constitutional Court. We refer to the judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union of 18 January 2018 - Ruiz Conejero case - in which a preliminary ruling on the compatibility of Article 52(d) of the Staff Regulations with Directive 2000/78 of 27 November 2000 on equal treatment in employment and, on the other hand, with Constitutional Court judgment 118/2019 of 16 October. It seems that we have not understood those resolutions in the same way. Both resolutions are cited in the Government's report, although it is true that it does so in search of ways to make them say just the opposite of what they say. Because what the Constitutional Court's ruling really says is that the repealed provision is neither unconstitutional nor in any way breaches the requirements of the said European directive, which has been in force for twenty years. Moreover, what the Constitutional Court has declared with reference to this precept is the following - I quote textual words -: the regulation contained in article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute responds to the legitimate objective of protecting the productivity of the company. Let us not forget that, from the legal point of view, there is a right of freedom of enterprise, which recognizes article 38 of the Spanish Constitution, and entrusts to the public authorities the guarantee and protection of their exercise, as well as the defence of productivity, which is a constitutional good, so that it is clear that both sentences are compelling arguments to maintain that precept. Taking into account that Article 54 of the Workers' Statute provides for disciplinary dismissal and that Article 52(d) excludes a number of cases of disability - serious illness, accidents at work, pregnancy -, the amendment focuses on the possibility of extinguishing a contract of employment when repeated and constant lack of assistance occurs, for what we call common contingencies or for dealing with particular matters supposedly inexcusable. We will not enter into the fraud that often occurs when these reasons are invoked, because that is not the meaning of the law. We all know that fraud occurs, but for that there is already the disciplinary dismissal of Article 54 already cited. We are going to assume that when a worker misses his job in the circumstances described, in most cases he does so for objective reasons not attributable to his conduct and therefore does not incur any fault. Let us assume once again that no one should be punished for this, but one thing is guilt and the other thing is to know who has to deal with the damage caused by the suspension of their labour obligations, because it is evident that the lack of assistance generates damage; first, the company, whose productivity is undermined, and that is the main purpose of precept 52.d), as established by the Constitutional Court; secondly, it also harms the rest of the workers, who have to assume the workload that the absent partner ceases to carry out. Madam Minister, I think we should all understand that within the framework of the regulation of labour relations there must be a balance between the three parties: companies, workers and the unemployed, because everyone has their rights and the law must protect them all, because we must ensure that workers and their stability are protected. We are not questioning this stability at all insofar as we are defending what the Workers' Statute says for the past 40 years. Companies, especially SMEs, cooperatives and self-employed, whose productivity depends on their future and that of the other workers who are linked to them, must also be taken care of. This makes it perfectly clear that the authors of this amendment are people outside the business world, because they are perfectly ignorant of the serious problem of absenteeism in business terms, even on a day-to-day basis, when colleagues have to take on the work of those who are often absent, and let us not talk about chain work. We cannot forget those who want to enter the labour market, the more than three million unemployed who have not yet had the opportunity to find a job and who you never seem to worry about; among them there are so many people waiting for an opportunity, and perhaps others are not in a position to take advantage of it, because they cannot or do not want to. Our employers and workers have shown that when they have adequate tools they are able to conserve and create jobs, and more intensely than any other country in Europe. Leave the demagogy behind. ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u16	Thank you very much, Madam President. I would like briefly to begin this, my first intervention in this session, with a memory and a recognition to all the people who are suffering in their flesh the pandemic of the coronavirus and, also, to those workers who are disabling themselves so that we can be here today and that all citizens can cope with this health crisis that we are experiencing in the best possible way. We discussed the validation of Royal Decree-Law 4/2020, repealing article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute, which, as has already been pointed out, considered as an objective cause of dismissal the lack of yet justified but intermittent work attendance, except for different cases of medical and non-medical layoffs, delinking them after the 2012 reform of the general rate of absenteeism in the company and adding before that reform of 2012, although at a minimum, a dose of proportionality to this form of dismissal for objective reasons. However, and despite the apparent hardness of the precept repealed by Royal Decree-Law 4/2020, its impact on labour relations during its existence was never significant due to the decisive review and mitigation of the case law to which it was subject, limitations and mitigations coming from both the Court of Justice of the European Union, the ECJ, and the State courts, and which, pursuant to Directive 2078, protected the prohibition of discrimination on grounds of disability, minimizing the specific applications of Article 52(d) of the Workers' Statute for the purpose of monitoring their adequacy with regard to the purpose of combating absenteeism and proportionality control, which prevented it from going beyond the purpose of combating absenteeism. However, this limited and mitigated incidence of the precept now repealed by the actual decree-law cited must not constitute an obstacle or demerit in order to articulate this ad hoc amendment in view of the social and media impact caused, so that our Basque Parliamentary Group will vote in favour of the validation of this decree-law, without prejudice - we also announce - to vote also in favour of its processing as a draft law in order to be able to consider, where appropriate, as regards the problem of absenteeism some relationship, perhaps close to that in force in the consolidated text of the 1995 Workers' Statute, to which some other exclusions of justified absences, both medical and non-medical, could be incorporated, to which were contemplated until the day of the promulgation of the decree whose validation we now discuss. Thank you very much, Madam President.

Ladies and gentlemen, the future of the nation, of this nation, has never been written and, of course, will not be written after this epidemic. When we open our doors again, when we go back to work normally, when we go back out, move naturally and visit our family regularly, when all this happens, nothing will be the same, everything will have changed. If I am certain of anything, the people of this country are much clearer about the importance of the public, which is the best shield a society can have to face the challenges we face as a country. Ladies and gentlemen, the Socialist Parliamentary Group will vote in favour of the validation of Royal Decree-Law 4/2020 of 18 February, repealing the objective dismissal for lack of labour assistance provided for in Article 52(d) of the consolidated text of the Workers' Statute Act. It will do so for a simple reason: this royal decree gives back to working people guarantees taken away by the labour reform of the People's Party in the face of dismissal. Ladies and gentlemen, let's be clear. The abrogated dismissal was not justified. Ladies and gentlemen, it is a pride to be able to intervene today in defence of this decree-law, as it is urgent to restore the rights of the working class. It is urgent to restore the legal certainty snatched by the reform that boosted the balance in the employment relationship; that allowed the substantial modification of working conditions practically unilaterally by the employer, broken and collective bargaining, and that also facilitated that the workers could be dismissed indiscriminately for lack of assistance, even though these are justified. A cause of dismissal that will repeal this royal decree that we are debating today. Ladies and gentlemen, with this royal decree the Government continues to dismantle the most damaging aspects of the labour reform of the People's Party, despite the fact that it weighs heavily. A reform whose result - unfortunately contrasted - is the loss of workers' rights. A loss of rights that is mainly the result of the loss of wages, the only formula that the People's Party understood to gain in competitiveness and that has led to the emergence of a new social class, the poor workers. The socialist government increased the interprofessional minimum wage to 900 euros, an increase of 22.3%, the largest increase in the history of our democracy. Today the interprofessional minimum wage is 950 euros. By the way, increases that have been made prior to social dialogue, something unknown by the governments of the People's Party. An increase that according to the economic gurus of the right was little less than to boost the national economic structure and that far from such a omen has only brought dignity and security to millions of Spanish households. Ladies and gentlemen, this royal decree-law restores safety to the working person who, suffering from illness or disability, was forced to fail intermittently to work even with justification. Those absences that did not depend on his will - I repeat: that did not depend on his will - constituted the cause of dismissal regardless of whether they were just cause. The dismissal took place automatically, an automatism provoked by the reform of the People's Party that deprived it of the elements that gave it objectivity, such as the index of total absenteeism of the staff. If the excuse of the right was to combat absenteeism, absenteeism is already constituting disciplinary dismissal, so there is no lack of protection of the business interest in any way. Ladies and gentlemen, this article made this business interest prevail in defending its captivity in the face of an objective and proportionate cause of dismissal. This, as I said, called into question a principle of our work order: dismissal is always due to cause. Otherwise it would violate the Community framework, and more specifically the Convention 158 of the International Labour Organization, ratified by Spain in 1985 and thus incorporated in our legal system, in accordance with article 96.1 of our Constitution. Ladies and gentlemen, this article only creates legal uncertainty, as it brings nothing but fear, demotivation and a decline in productivity among working people. Look, in less than a hundred days this government is fulfilling its commitment to repealing the most harmful aspects of labour reform; the repeal that we are debating today is only the beginning of that process. The right will say that this reform is going to end the economic recovery that that same right has wanted to appropriate as its own by stealing it from the efforts and suffering of the working people, of the humblest people in this country. They shall hide the fact that this derogation is made for three main reasons. Firstly, because it generated legal uncertainty, since it was an abnormal legal figure corrected over the years precisely to exclude absences that could lead to dismissal incompatible with the basic rights of workers, such as work accidents, maternity leave, holidays or exclusions from gender-based violence; something totally incomprehensible. Secondly, because it is contrary to Community law, as established by the Court of Justice of the European Union in its judgment of 18 January 2018, which states that the automatic application of Article 52(d) without the existence of mechanisms designed to counter and assess the concrete situation of dismissal and to analyse it on a case-by-case basis for the purpose of determining its proportionality is incompatible with Council Directive 2000/78. And finally, because it is discriminatory, because it makes the most vulnerable people worse when they are sick, something we can agree, ladies and gentlemen, it does not seek anyone intentionally not to go to work. This discrimination is particularly harsh with persons with disabilities or with people with chronic basic diseases, who are more likely to have more short-term medical discharges, precisely as a result of these pathologies - and I am finishing, Madam President -. In addition, it constitutes discrimination on the basis of gender, since it is women who essentially assume the work of caring for dependent persons and because of it may incur a greater number of short-term absences from work. Ladies and gentlemen, I conclude. In these days, if there is something important that we are checking is that health was, is and will always be the most important. Solidarity, fraternity, equity and equality; we have the opportunity once again from this House to do social justice again, being, from my humble point of view, the only just path and the only path that will never return. Thank you so much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u36	We turn to the agenda item on the validation or repeal of Royal Decree-Law 5/2020 of 25 February, adopting certain urgent measures in the field of agriculture and food. To present the royal decree-law, the Minister of Agriculture, Fisheries and Food has the floor on behalf of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u37	Thank you very much, Madam President. Allow me at the beginning of my speech to make my own statements at the beginning of this session of friendship and solidarity addressed to the families and friends of all those who died from this pandemic and to all those who are suffering it. Ladies and gentlemen, I appear before this House to ask on behalf of the Government for the validation of Royal Decree-Law 5/2020, adopting certain urgent measures in the field of agriculture and food, adopted by the Council of Ministers on 25 February. It is a necessary and indefatigable royal decree-law for the agri-food sector of our country and, in particular, for our farmers and livestock farmers. In addition, the conditions of extraordinary and urgent need established by our Constitution to adopt this legal norm with the status of law are at stake. Madam President, before setting out its contents, I would like to reflect on and acknowledge it. I'm starting with reflection. Farmers, farmers and fishermen are producing and placing on the market fresh products that enable the supply of industry and distribution and, consequently, the food of all. In recent weeks I have repeatedly explained how important your work is to society as a whole. In a dramatic and extreme circumstance like the one we are living in, his work is even more valued. We need them, also the workers of the cooperative companies and the agri-food industry. Its effort is also enabling the chain to function smoothly and that basic food can reach everyone. Finally, I would like to mention who is closest to all of us on a day-to-day basis, the commercial distribution, the shops and supermarkets that supply us and all those who provide services in them. The role that commercial distribution, businesses and workers are playing is crucial for all food products to reach all citizens, not forgetting logistics and supply companies, without which the chain could not function. I would therefore like to make a public recognition on behalf of the Government of Spain on the good work of all those who serve in the agri-food chain. Today, more than an effort, it is a sign of surrender and solidarity, and this allows us to be supplied every day in sufficient quantity and quality with practical normality, despite the exceptional circumstances that we are experiencing. Again, thank you. In this context, the rebalancing of the food chain, its transparency in the formation of prices and the improvement of the negotiating position of farmers and farmers in the search for fairer prices is all the more justified and necessary. Indeed, in recent months a number of factors have converged, some of them of a cyclical and others of a structural nature, dragged away from good times ago, which have led to a crisis situation in the prices perceived by our farmers and farmers in their sales and, consequently, in the decline of their business margins. This has prompted the prompt response of this Government, which has taken concrete action urgently in response to the demands of the sector and the agricultural professional organizations that represent them. A sector which is in itself structurally vulnerable by its own characteristics, by the high rigidity of demand, by the atomisation of operators and the diversity of its agents against a much more concentrated purchase, as well as by seasonality and, in many cases, by the perishable nature of their productions. To all these singularities, ladies and gentlemen, we must add the concurrence of short-term factors, concrete situations and events which have suffered and suffered from our farmers and farmers, which have been affected by extreme weather phenomena which have caused considerable damage, in the form of storms or floods, and also by measures taken for reasons which have nothing to do with the agri-food sector, such as the tariffs adopted by the United States, the fall in prices or the increase in the costs of agricultural inputs. These situations require decisive action by the Government of Spain and the adoption of solutions that cannot be delayed for another day because of the damage that this could cause. We have heard, discussed and acted without delay, following that process of dialogue with all actors in the food chain and with the autonomous communities, with a real decree-law that aims to achieve a positive and necessary impact by promoting equitable sharing of value added and the rebalancing of the food chain, improving the negotiating capacity of the weakest links in the chain and strengthening the competitiveness of agricultural and food operators. For this reason, the royal decree-law that I present to you contains a series of measures that, succinctly, I begin to report. First, Act No. 12/2013 on measures to improve the food chain is amended. This implies, from the date of entry into force and its publication on 26 February last, the obligation to include the actual cost of production in agricultural contracts, which expressly states that the agreed price covers the actual cost of production. Secondly, it is proposed to prohibit the destruction of the value of products along the chain, which is popularly referred to as loss-making. That is to say, to oblige each operator to pay the immediately preceding one in the chain a price equal to or higher than the cost of production of the product in which that operator has incurred or assumed. Thirdly, it is proposed to regulate commercial promotions to avoid the trivialization of agricultural products and food, ensuring that society is aware of its true value, and requiring that promotional activities be deployed within an equitable framework of obligations and rights within the chain. Fourthly, it is intended to publicise the penalties imposed on non-compliance and to tighten the sanctioning regime, such as, for example, that failure to formalise food contracts in writing becomes a serious breach rather than a minor one as it has been so far. This regulation, ladies and gentlemen, will be supplemented in an imminent manner by a second amendment of the law of the wider chain, with a draft law transposing Directive 633/2019 of unfair commercial practices and introducing new measures to improve the functioning of the food chain. The incorporation of young people into agricultural activity is one of the essential lines of the policy of renewal of the primary sector and one of the axes on which the new common agricultural policy will be articulated, and to this end the field must be attractive to young people, but above all it must be profitable. That is the key to the future of agriculture, livestock and our rural world. It is therefore proposed in this Decree-Law to amend Article 2 of Law 35/2006 on the income tax of natural persons to incorporate a measure to facilitate the four-year assessment of the tax on the first installation of young farmers provided for in the National Rural Development Programme, so that they do not have to do so once in the first year, including the partners of the associations. The decree-law also regulates the reduction from 35 to 20 of the number of peonadas necessary to access the unemployment benefit or agricultural income in the Autonomous Communities of Andalusia and Extremadura. The last marketing year saw a decline in olive production due to climatic circumstances, which, together with the difficulties caused by the tariffs I mentioned earlier, has had an impact on a decrease in the employment of potential agricultural workers in these two autonomous communities. Similarly, in order to encourage undertakings to convert any agricultural contracts into fixed and fixed discontinuous contracts, bonuses shall be granted in the business share according to the form and amount of the base and the contribution group in which the worker is discharged during the two years following the conversion of the contract. Finally, the Decree-Law provides for the amendment of Law 23/2015, the computer of the system of labour inspection and social security, in order to extend the scope of the inspection beyond the workplace, i.e. that its performance can also be carried out in premises and dwellings or other authorized places where workers reside, stay or remain. I conclude, ladies and gentlemen. This state of alarm in Spain is somehow a useful parenthesis for reflection from which lessons can be drawn, and one of the clearest is that we need our farmers and livestock farmers. Also our fishermen, who are always there, doing their job well. I therefore ask you to vote for the validation of this royal decree-law, which is part of a task and commitment of this government that will continue throughout the legislature to improve the functioning of our primary sector, allow the survival of a living rural environment and recognize and dignify the work of our farmers and livestock farmers. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u41	Madam President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, the state has for a long time been regulating the food chain and its impact on agricultural markets with urgent measures. Note the contrast between 'from a long time ago' and 'emergency measures'. In other words, the rules themselves tell us that they are late to intervene in a very complex sector such as the agricultural sector. However, it is true that the successive rules, as is now the case with this royal decree-law, have tried to meet the growing social demands of farmers through measures that set conditions in markets of origin to the consumer and in different contracts of agricultural and livestock products. During this crisis that we are experiencing we have been able to see that - as you have said - despite this important regulatory omission for its activity, the agricultural sector plays a fundamental social and economic role in the Spanish territory. It is a basic sector for the economy, as it is an indispensable part for the food of the whole country, as highlighted by the situation we live in, but, moreover, it is an economic activity that is not mobile: any aid you receive is going to stay here, it is not going to go anywhere else. Farmers are a social group with a deep commitment to collaborate unselfishly in emergency situations: they have done so in the snow, with the spraying now of public roads, with emergencies, in the fight against fire and we have seen it in these past few weeks. We value the proposals of this Royal Decree 5/2020, which aims to clarify the successive markets that, through food contracts, are integrated into the value chain, avoiding that the actual cost of production is not covered, as very well says article 12 of the decree on the destruction of value in chain. The explanatory statement of the royal decree speaks of specific regulatory measures in the field of the food chain, anticipating a future in-depth reform, which is planned to be undertaken later, of Law 12/2013, of measures for the improvement of the food chain. We agree with that assessment. What is important and necessary is an in-depth reform of Law 12/2013. Perhaps the present royal decree could have gone further in some of the aspects that we are going to point out; however, despite these limitations, we will support its validation, because we believe that it represents a step forward on the previous situation and that it facilitates the viability of agricultural enterprises. Despite what we say, we believe that the measures of the Royal Decree will not be sufficient to guarantee the objectives of the Code of Good Practice in Food Procurement for many reasons. There are three of them that we believe should be incorporated: first, the code of good practice itself - which would strengthen its applicability - should be strengthened by measures that make their implementation easy, because there are difficulties in practical and real implementation; secondly, specific measures should be taken to ensure the transparency of those food chain business practices and of the requirements laid down by commercial contracts; and thirdly, it is important that the whole marketing process be made accessible and synthetic to consumers and that it is not now left in the darkness of an unknown chain. The introduction of transparency criteria in all successive chain trade agreements and the application of rapid and efficient information mechanisms should enable the consumer to identify the commercial traceability of the product he acquires and to define it. This royal decree can also anticipate specific measures for the future reform of Law 12/2013. For us, the main thing is to strengthen information about the origin of the products, defining their characteristics and the conditions of their production. In order for consumers to be able to choose, they should establish categories to be reflected in the Code of Business Practices. We believe that it is very important to differentiate between national products, European products which comply with European rules, non-European products which comply with European rules on fertilisers, feed, plant protection products, animal health products and, finally, products which are not controlled but which are on the market. This royal decree should apply the conditions to products in accordance with the production system and also to distance and transport. It could clearly differentiate between local product - 100 kilometers from the market -, proximity product - between 100 and 250-, medium distance product - between 250 and 700-; it could also differentiate the transport that has suffered the product or the environmental impact that that transport has had, so that the labelling collects its information in a synthetic and graphical way. We believe that national products are not competing on equal terms with those imported. There is no competition on equal terms because here there are labour guarantees that are not available in third countries of which it is imported; there is no competition on equal terms, because here there are products that are banned while in other countries are being used; there is no competition on equal costs: inputs are higher here than in third countries, both in fertilizers and in feed and in plant protection products. It is necessary to ensure that products imported into the European Union meet the same quality, safety and rights requirements as those required in production within the European Union. The Royal Decree should include some urgent action against fraud in labelling to remedy the lack of information for consumers in the shops. On the other hand, we want to say that it is very difficult for all producers to have contracts for all agricultural products. As you know, a very common practice is the sale in long, and in long the price is fixed at the time of the purchase - usually the supplier operates at a percentage of that sale -, with which it is very difficult to be collected in a contract. It is also difficult to determine what the reference production cost is, because it is not the same for a farmer who manages 6 hectares as for a 400-hectare farm; it is not the same for a warehouse that handles 4 million kilos as for a warehouse that handles 100 million kilos. We suggest that these numerals be introduced through a segmentation of the criteria of both the producer's and the warehouseman's size and the quality of the product. In short, for us the royal decree should adopt an urgent measure on transparency and information. A product classification must be created that allows the consumer, when in the supermarket, to quickly identify whether the product is national, if it is in compliance with European rules, which is very easy to establish with colour stamps that graphically identify the origin and quality of the product, as has often been proposed: green stamp for national products, blue stamp for the European Union, orange stamp for products outside the European Union but which comply with its rules and without seals those which are not classified. There is another additional issue, also with regard to the third article, as regards the reduction of the minimum number of actual days. We propose that there be a system, a sort of bag of hours, where the hours of more worked in a few days are compensated for the days that are not worked, and at the end of the campaign an equivalence is made between the hours worked in that bag...

ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u49	Thank you very much, Madam President. From our point of view, all contractual relations between the operators of the chain should be bound by the rules laid down in this decree-law and should not affect only those operators who voluntarily adhere. Of course, this also affects the actual capacity to sanction non-compliance. However, the most serious and damaging aspect for Galicia is its centralizing character. It does not take the opportunity to change legislation with regard to the regulation of interprofessionals as being of a supraautonomic nature, thus preventing Galicia from constituting its own interprofessionals. In our case, it is a relevant issue that affects the main productions of our country: milk, meat, wine, vegetable production, etc., but it is especially significant in the case of the dairy sector, since Galicia is the first milk power of the Spanish State, producing around 40% and accounting for 50% of the farms producing. Therefore, in view of these considerations, our position will be abstaining. Thank you.

The first: Will this change in the food chain also extend to the intermediaries of the national network of markets, Mercasa, participated in equal parts between the ministry and the municipalities, which today still operates outside the law with contracts of deposit or sale as a result? And the second: When, once and for all, will the transposition of European Directive 2019/633 of 17 April 2019 on commercial, unfair and abusive practices be processed? We believe that its application is peremptory, this directive precisely comes to an end to the abuse of a deposit contract and that it prevents the sale as a result, recognizing something as simple as that any agri-food trade must be based on a contract and a price. With regard to the amendment of Act No. 35/2006 of 28 November 2006 on the taxation of natural persons and the partial amendment of corporate taxes, income of non-residents and property, we continue to value positively the contributions made and the Royal Decree introduces amendments to improve the taxation of young farmers who receive assistance in their first installation within the rural development programmes of the autonomous communities, something crucial to promote rejuvenation and generational relief so essential in the sector. However, it should be remembered that the producer sector has been demanding various cost reduction measures in order to be able to overcome the current campaign and, in view of the new income declaration, we propose to adapt the modules of the IRPF to the loss of income suffered securely. In short, we understand that it is a good royal decree; it can benefit many people and, therefore, we will vote in favour. Since we believe that the measures presented are as necessary as they are insufficient, we urge that this royal decree be used as a bill so that the necessary contributions can be made. We must move towards a model of agricultural exploitation that allows social, economic, territorial and environmental development. We must give a local response to all these global challenges. Thank you very much.

Let me make a few reflections on the Community's agricultural policy. Agricultural and livestock production in Spain is mediated and conditioned by the common agricultural policy. The CAP was designed and put at the service of private interests, because, first, the distribution of CAP funds in Spain favours large farms compared to small and medium-sized farmers. Secondly, the CAP benefits land ownership in relation to production, so a large part of the aid is directed not at the real food producers but at the landowners. The CAP implies the forced application of European producers of schemes and methodologies in practices and on their plant protection, socio-laboral and environmental productions, which have very laudable and necessary objectives but which do not apply equally to productions imported from third countries through the free trade agreements; and here let me ask you that in this context in which we are reviewing this refusal to call for the veto of citrus trees in South Africa which is so much affecting farmers in Levante. I believe that in the exceptional situation that we are in, perhaps, it is time to ask for that safeguard clause. Fourthly, all this results in a perverse and fraudulent system by which all that the large intermediary companies are saving is being paid with public aid. In other words, large food multinationals get their multi-million dollar profits at the expense of taxpayers' money and at the expense of the impoverishment of small producers. The result of all this, ladies and gentlemen, is a growing process of concentration which only favours large capitals. We talk about large international operators of agribusiness, plant protection control, genetic patents of seeds, fertilizers or herbicides, and on the other hand the process of intermediaries that market the products. This situation leaves our producers indefensible about the abuses and impositions of these operators, and makes marginal issues such as food security, environmental conservation or the fair distribution of wealth. This is what causes the concept of free market and free trade, and it is what the policy that has been developed in recent years leads us to a market of imperfect competition and unfair competition from large forbearers and producers over small, different levels of the food chain, from foreign production to local production. The problem is structural and it is not only in Spain, it happens all over the world, it is a problem of economic model, model of property, production, distribution and consumption. It is, at last, the defence of its own dignity, the dignity of a professional sector, the primary sector, which is key to this Government and to the economy of this country, a sector that maintains most of the economic and social life of our territory, of our peoples, of rural Spain, a Spain that maintains the demographic risks of depopulation and ageing. This primary sector is a guarantee for this Government of the social and territorial cohesion of our Spain, a sector that, as I have been told by those who have preceded me in this rostrum, is proving to be key and strategic for the food supply to the entire population in one of the most difficult and complicated times for Spain and for those of us who live in it. Farmers, farmers and their agricultural professional organizations described their situation as a perfect storm just two months ago, where we all recognized that the reduction in trade and economic margins was due, as the Minister of Agriculture has explained, to various factors, which I will not repeat again, from the adverse weather phenomena of this season to the United States Brexit or tariffs, but we could also talk about our own configuration in a trade negotiation carried out on many occasions by a retail sector. However, ladies and gentlemen, from the very first moment the president of the government, Pedro Sánchez, expressed his support for the just demands of farmers and farmers, ordered and put to work his entire government, and they have done so from the various ministries, led in the first place by Luis Planas, Minister of Agriculture, Fisheries and Food, who in the framework of the table of agricultural dialogue with professional organizations has maintained a work of dialogue, empathy and, why not say so too, minister, of negotiation - right? - of negotiation. The response given by the Government has therefore been the result of dialogue and negotiation with the various sectors, with their legitimate representatives, with the agricultural organizations, but also with the distribution chains and with the autonomous communities, as well as with areas of competence in the various fields. This has also been understood by the Socialist Parliamentary Group, which during all these months has maintained a permanent dialogue in its territories with farmers, livestock and fishermen from the leadership of the parliamentary group and its spokesman, Adriana Lastra, and the various Members on its territory. As you know, the royal decree includes different measures, a whole package of measures and bonuses, in which I will not stop, although let me highlight three that I consider relevant, important. The first, without doubt, is the mandatory clause in contracts, a mandatory clause that will give legal certainty to the extent that it is to reflect the price agreed between farmers and farmers and their first buyer covering production costs; therefore, legal certainty. In the second one, I would like to highlight the subject of taxation. It is true that amendments are being made to the IRPF Act to improve the taxation of young people in this tax; yes, of young people who receive aid for the first installation in the rural development programmes established in the different autonomous communities, because you know well, ladies and gentlemen, that generational relief is a pressing issue in the agriculture and livestock sector. In the coming years they will retire, and Minister Planas repeats it on many occasions, six out of ten current farmers. The generational relief is pressing to move the primary sector forward in Spain. And the third of them is undoubtedly in the field of employment. Yes, the number of paid days is reduced to a minimum and it will therefore be easier to access the unemployment benefit - I am finishing, Madam President -. These are times when more than ever we are called to unity, responsibility, we are called to collaboration and cooperation. Therefore, on behalf of the Socialist Parliamentary Group, I ask you to vote in favour of the validation of this royal decree. Without delay and without delay, these measures must be implemented as soon as possible. You're going to allow me, President, to consume one more minute. As a Member of Parliament who I am for Madrid, allow me not to abandon this rostrum without remembering, without dedicating words of affection and recognition to Madrid, to the neighbors of Madrid, to its towns and cities, to those who suffer with the loss of those who want most, to those who are sick, to those who are concerned about their relatives and to recognize thousands of women and men who care for us and protect us every day and every night. Be my words of gratitude and gratitude and also my extended feelings for all of Spain. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2020-03-25-CD200325.u64	Thank you very much, Madam President.
